========== INPUT [doctor] ==========
[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.2, F48.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess it’s mostly that I’ve been having a hard time getting past how I look. My face and hands, especially. They just seem so rough and worn out to me—cracks, spots, sagging, that kind of thing.\n\nI know that probably sounds vain, and my wife tells me I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors, but when I’m around bankers or county office people, I feel like they’re looking right at it. Like that’s all they can see. I’m not sure if that’s the kind of thing you help with, but it’s been bothering me enough that I figured I should come in.\n\nDoctor: Has this concern about your appearance been affecting your daily life or relationships in any way?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… I mean, I still get things done, mostly. But there have been times I’ve missed calls with bankers or put off making farm decisions because I couldn’t quite get myself looking, you know, acceptable enough to sit down with people.\n\nAnd I’ve stopped saying yes to photos for the retreat and the craft sales, even though I know those would help. It feels silly saying it out loud, but it does get in the way some.\n\nDoctor: Is it just your face and hands that you're concerned about, or are there other parts of your body that bother you as well?\n\nPatient: Mostly my face and hands, yes. I mean… those are the things I can’t seem to get past. The cracks and spots on my hands, and my face looking kind of saggy or worn out.\n\nI don’t want to make too much of it, but when I’m talking to a banker or someone from the county, it feels like that’s what they’re noticing right away. My wife says it’s just from working outdoors, so maybe I’m overthinking it… but those are the parts that really bother me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your appearance is actually abnormal, or is it just that you feel self-conscious about it?\n\nPatient: I guess… I don’t know if I’d say “abnormal,” exactly. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. My wife says I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors, and maybe that’s true.\n\nBut to me, my face and hands look really rough and worn—cracks and spots on my hands, my face looking saggy—and I have a hard time getting past it. When I’m sitting with a banker or someone from the county, it feels like they’re staring right at those things, even if maybe they aren’t. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think other people actually notice these flaws in your appearance, or is it just that you feel self-conscious about them?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say it’s mostly just me being self-conscious… and maybe some of it is. My wife keeps telling me I just look like someone who’s worked outdoors a long time.\n\nBut honestly, when I’m sitting across from a banker or someone from the county, I can’t get past the feeling that they’re noticing it—the cracks and spots on my hands, my face looking saggy and worn. It feels like that’s what they’re looking at, even if I know I might be reading too much into it. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your appearance is really as rough and worn out as you feel it is, or is it just that you feel that way about it?\n\nPatient: I suppose… it’s probably not as bad as it feels to me. I don’t want to make it sound like I think I’m some kind of monster or anything.\n\nBut it’s hard for me to shake it. My face and hands just look so rough and worn to me—the cracks, the spots, the sagging—and when I’m across from a banker or someone from the county, I feel like that’s what they’re seeing. My wife says I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors, and maybe she’s right… but I can’t seem to get past it. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about the cracks, spots, or sagging that bothers you the most?\n\nPatient: I think it’s the whole… worn-out look, if that makes sense. The cracks and spots on my hands bother me because they feel really obvious when I’m signing papers or shaking someone’s hand. And with my face, it’s the sagging—like I look tired and rough all the time.\n\nI know my wife says I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors, and I try to take that in. But when I’m with a banker or someone from the county, I can’t stop thinking they’re staring at those things. That’s really what brings me in, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel better about your appearance if the specific issues with your face and hands, like the cracks and spots, were somehow resolved?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, I’d probably feel better if those things weren’t so noticeable. At least that’s what I tell myself.\n\nWhat really brings me in is that I can’t get past how rough and worn my face and hands look. The cracks, the spots, the sagging… when I’m with a banker or someone from the county, I feel like that’s what they’re staring at, even though my wife says I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors.\n\nMaybe if those specific things were fixed, I’d relax some. I hope that’s a reasonable way to put it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're ugly or unattractive in general, or is it just these specific issues with your face and hands that bother you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say I feel ugly overall… at least, I don’t want to put it that strongly. It’s more those specific things, my face and hands looking so rough and worn.\n\nWhat brings me in is I can’t seem to get past the cracks and spots on my hands, and the sagging in my face. When I’m dealing with a banker or someone from the county, I get this feeling like that’s what they’re noticing, even though my wife tells me I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors. I know that may sound a little much, but that’s where my mind goes.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel self-conscious about your appearance if people complimented you or told you that you look fine?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say yes, that I could just take the compliment and let it go. And I do try to, especially when my wife tells me I look fine or that I just look like someone who’s worked outdoors.\n\nBut honestly… I think I’d still feel self-conscious. What brings me in is that I can’t seem to get past how rough and worn my face and hands look. If I’m with a banker or someone from the county, I start thinking they’re looking at the cracks and spots on my hands, or the sagging in my face, even if nobody says anything.\n\nI know that may not be entirely fair or reasonable, but that’s how it feels to me.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about your appearance caused you to avoid social or work-related activities most of the time?\n\nPatient: Not most of the time, I don’t think… I mean, I still get things done. But yes, it has gotten in the way more than I’d like to admit.\n\nI’ve missed calls with bankers because I couldn’t quite get myself to feel presentable enough to sit down and talk with them. And I’ve put off some farm decisions for the same reason, which sounds foolish when I say it out loud.\n\nI’ve also stopped agreeing to be in photos for the retreat or the craft sales, even when I know the pictures would probably help promote things. I just… I don’t want my face or hands showing up that way. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your appearance is actually as bad as you feel it is, or is it just your perception of it?\n\nPatient: I suppose… if I’m being honest, it’s probably partly my perception. My wife says I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors, and I know she’s not trying to just flatter me.\n\nBut it doesn’t feel that way in the moment. What brings me in is that I can’t get past how rough and worn my face and hands look. I start feeling like every banker or county person is staring at the cracks and spots on my hands, or the sagging in my face.\n\nI know that may sound stronger than it really is… but that’s where my mind goes.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your perception of your appearance might be different from how others actually see you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it probably is different. I mean, I hope it is.\n\nMy wife tells me I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors, and I know she means that kindly and probably honestly. But in my own head, I can’t get past how rough and worn my face and hands look. When I’m around a banker or someone from the county, I start thinking they’re noticing the cracks, the spots, the sagging… even if they haven’t said a word.\n\nSo yes, I guess my perception may be stronger than what other people are actually seeing. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think that if you were to change your appearance to look less rough and worn, people would actually treat you differently?\n\nPatient: I… I suppose part of me does think that, yes. Maybe not in a dramatic way, but if my face and hands looked less rough, less worn out, I think I’d feel like people were taking me more seriously.\n\nEspecially bankers or county people — I get this idea they’re looking at the cracks in my hands, the spots, the sagging in my face, and judging me before I even say anything. My wife tells me I just look like someone who’s worked outdoors, and I know that’s probably fair.\n\nBut I can’t quite get past it. So yes, I think I imagine they’d treat me differently… though I know that may be more in my head than anything they’re actually doing. Is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you think that other people actually see the flaws in your appearance that you're concerned about?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, I do worry they see them. Maybe not as much as I imagine, but that’s what it feels like.\n\nWhat brings me in is I can’t seem to get past how rough and worn my face and hands look. If I’m talking to a banker or someone from the county, I start thinking they’re staring at the cracks in my hands, the spots, the sagging in my face… even if they haven’t actually said anything.\n\nMy wife says I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors, and I know she’s probably right. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. But in my head, it feels like those things are obvious to everyone. Does that answer it okay?\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about your appearance ever caused you to avoid important work or social events?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, not constantly, but it has gotten in the way some.\n\nI’ve missed calls with bankers, or put them off, because I couldn’t get myself to feel like I looked acceptable enough to sit down and talk with them. And that’s embarrassing to admit, because it sounds foolish when I say it out loud. There’ve been farm decisions I delayed too, because I didn’t want to deal with people seeing me like that.\n\nAnd, socially or for the business side of things… I’ve stopped agreeing to photos that might help promote the retreat or the craft sales. I tell myself it’s not a big thing, but I know it probably doesn’t help.\n\nIs that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your appearance is actually as flawed as you perceive it to be, or is it possible that your perception is exaggerated?\n\nPatient: I think… it’s probably exaggerated some. I can say that here, at least. My wife tells me I look like a man who’s worked outdoors, not like something is wrong with me.\n\nBut when I’m actually around people — bankers, county folks, people like that — it feels very real. I get stuck on my hands and face, the cracks, the spots, the sagging, and I’m almost sure they’re noticing it right away.\n\nSo… maybe it’s not as bad as I perceive it. I want to believe that. I just can’t seem to get past how rough and worn I look. Is that an okay way to put it?\n\nDoctor: Do you believe your face and hands actually appear as rough and worn out to others as they do to you?\n\nPatient: I suppose… not exactly as bad as it looks to me. I can admit that.\n\nBut it feels that way in the moment. When I’m sitting with a banker or someone from the county, I get stuck thinking they’re looking right at the cracks in my hands, the spots on my face, the sagging… all of it. My wife says I just look like someone who’s worked outdoors his whole life, and I know that’s probably the kinder, more realistic way to see it.\n\nStill, I can’t quite shake the feeling that it’s obvious to other people. I don’t know if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your appearance is actually as rough and worn out as you feel it is, or is that just how you perceive it?\n\nPatient: I think… it’s partly how I perceive it, yes. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nBut what brings me in is that I can’t seem to get past it — how rough and worn my face and hands look to me. The cracks, the spots, the sagging… when I’m with a banker or someone from the county, it feels like that’s what they’re staring at.\n\nMy wife says I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors, and I know that’s probably fair. I just… I don’t always feel it that way. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
